I know that probably sounds dramatic… but that’s what happens in my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself sick, use laxatives, or exercise excessively to control your weight or keep from gaining any weight?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so. I mean, I don’t make myself sick or anything like that. And laxatives—no, that’s not something I do.\n\nExercise… I do yoga, but I wouldn’t call it excessive. It helps me feel settled. I know maybe other people might see things differently, but I’m not trying to do anything extreme. Is that… is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you lost more than 10% of your body weight in the past six months, either intentionally or without trying?\n\nPatient: I’m… not totally sure about the percentage. I don’t really calculate it that way.\n\nI know my weight has gone down some over the last few months, but I don’t know if it’s more than 10%. I could check, if that would help. I don’t want to give you the wrong answer.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me approximately how much weight you’ve lost in the past six months, and whether it’s been 10% or more of your body weight?\n\nPatient: I’m sorry, I don’t really know the exact number. I haven’t been keeping it in a way where I can say, like, “it’s this many pounds.”\n\nI know it’s gone down, and people have noticed, but whether it’s 10% or more… I don’t want to guess and give you the wrong answer. I could look back at my weights if that would be okay.\n\nDoctor: Would it be okay if we looked back at your weight measurements together to see if the amount you’ve lost in the past six months is around 10% or more of your body weight?\n\nPatient: Yes… that would be okay.\n\nI mean, occupational health is actually the reason I’m here—they were saying my weight is dangerously low for the kind of work I do, running presses and doing fabrication all day. I honestly thought I was just getting leaner, or more disciplined, not… dangerous.\n\nSo yes, we can look at the measurements together. I’d probably feel better not trying to estimate it on my own, if that’s alright.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I’m sorry… I don’t think I can honestly confirm the 10% number myself.

What I can say is that occupational health sent me here because they said my weight is dangerously low for someone doing my job—running presses and fabrication all day. I didn’t really think of it that way. I just thought I was getting leaner, more disciplined, maybe.

So… if the measurements show that it’s 10% or more, then I’ll accept that, but I don’t want to overstate it without you checking the numbers.

I just try to keep things controlled. Most days before the early shift I’ll just have coffee, and then maybe half a protein bar at lunch if I’m feeling like I need it. After yoga I’ll have a small bowl of vegetables.\n\nIt’s not like I’m trying to make anyone worry. It’s just… if I eat more than that, the whole day starts to feel out of control. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you consider yourself to be overweight or do you have a fear of gaining weight, even though others might tell you that you're underweight?\n\nPatient: I don’t really think of myself as… overweight, exactly. I know people are saying I’m too thin.\n\nBut if the scale goes up even a little, I get really scared. Like I’ve lost the control I worked so hard to keep, and I’m just going to keep gaining and end up heavy again. I know that probably sounds dramatic… but that’s what happens in my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself sick, use laxatives, or exercise excessively to control your weight or keep from gaining any weight?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so. I mean, I don’t make myself sick or anything like that. And laxatives—no, that’s not something I do.\n\nExercise… I do yoga, but I wouldn’t call it excessive. It helps me feel settled. I know maybe other people might see things differently, but I’m not trying to do anything extreme. Is that… is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you lost more than 10% of your body weight in the past six months, either intentionally or without trying?\n\nPatient: I’m… not totally sure about the percentage. I don’t really calculate it that way.\n\nI know my weight has gone down some over the last few months, but I don’t know if it’s more than 10%. I could check, if that would help. I don’t want to give you the wrong answer.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me approximately how much weight you’ve lost in the past six months, and whether it’s been 10% or more of your body weight?\n\nPatient: I’m sorry, I don’t really know the exact number. I haven’t been keeping it in a way where I can say, like, “it’s this many pounds.”\n\nI know it’s gone down, and people have noticed, but whether it’s 10% or more… I don’t want to guess and give you the wrong answer. I could look back at my weights if that would be okay.\n\nDoctor: Would it be okay if we looked back at your weight measurements together to see if the amount you’ve lost in the past six months is around 10% or more of your body weight?\n\nPatient: Yes… that would be okay.\n\nI mean, occupational health is actually the reason I’m here—they were saying my weight is dangerously low for the kind of work I do, running presses and doing fabrication all day. I honestly thought I was just getting leaner, or more disciplined, not… dangerous.\n\nSo yes, we can look at the measurements together. I’d probably feel better not trying to estimate it on my own, if that’s alright.\n\nDoctor: Can you confirm if your weight loss over the past six months has been 10% or more of your body weight, based on the measurements we can review?\n\nPatient: I… I can’t really confirm it myself just from memory. If we have the measurements in front of us, I’m okay looking at them with you, but I don’t want to say “yes” if I’m not sure.\n\nI know it’s been enough that people are concerned, but the 10% part… I’d rather you help me figure that out from the numbers, if that’s alright.\n\nDoctor: Can we look at your weight measurements together now to determine if your weight loss over the past six months has been 10% or more of your body weight?\n\nPatient: Yes, we can look at them now.\n\nI’m… a little embarrassed, honestly, because occupational health already made a big deal out of it. They said my weight is dangerously low for someone running presses and doing fabrication all day. I didn’t really see it that way—I thought I was just getting leaner, more disciplined, I guess.\n\nBut yes, if you think looking at the numbers together is the right thing, I’m okay with that.\n\nDoctor: Can you confirm whether your weight loss over the past six months has been 10% or more of your original body weight, based on the measurements we have?\n\nPatient: I’m sorry… I don’t think I can honestly confirm the 10% number myself.\n\nWhat I can say is that occupational health sent me here because they said my weight is dangerously low for someone doing my job—running presses and fabrication all day. I didn’t really think of it that way. I just thought I was getting leaner, more disciplined, maybe.\n\nSo… if the measurements show that it’s 10% or more, then I’ll accept that, but I don’t want to overstate it without you checking the numbers.\n\nDoctor: Based on the measurements we have, can you confirm whether your weight loss over the past six months has been 10% or more of your original body weight?\n\nPatient: I’m sorry, I still don’t think I can confirm that myself.\n\nIf the measurements you have show it’s 10% or more, then… I guess that’s what it is. I’m not trying to be difficult—I just don’t want to give the wrong answer or make it sound worse than it is. Could you tell me what the numbers show?\n\nDoctor: Based on the measurements we reviewed together, has your weight loss over the past six months been 10% or more of your original body weight?\n\nPatient: I… I know occupational health said it was dangerously low, especially for the kind of work I do with the presses and fabrication. I guess I didn’t really see it that way at first—I thought I was just getting leaner, more controlled.\n\nSo if the measurements we reviewed show it’s 10% or more, then… yes, I can accept that. I still feel a little embarrassed saying it, but I’m not trying to argue with the numbers.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I mean… I wouldn’t say I never eat, exactly. I do try to keep it pretty controlled, because otherwise I start feeling like the whole day is getting away from me.

Most days it’s coffee before the early shift, then maybe half a protein bar at lunch, and later, after yoga, I’ll have a small bowl of vegetables. If I eat more than that, even if it’s not that much more, I get really anxious—like I’ve messed up or lost control somehow.

I know that probably sounds a bit extreme when I say it out loud. I’m not trying to be difficult… it just feels safer when I keep it small.

If we have the measurements in front of us, I’m okay looking at them with you, but I don’t want to say “yes” if I’m not sure.\n\nI know it’s been enough that people are concerned, but the 10% part… I’d rather you help me figure that out from the numbers, if that’s alright.\n\nDoctor: Can we look at your weight measurements together now to determine if your weight loss over the past six months has been 10% or more of your body weight?\n\nPatient: Yes, we can look at them now.\n\nI’m… a little embarrassed, honestly, because occupational health already made a big deal out of it. They said my weight is dangerously low for someone running presses and doing fabrication all day. I didn’t really see it that way—I thought I was just getting leaner, more disciplined, I guess.\n\nBut yes, if you think looking at the numbers together is the right thing, I’m okay with that.\n\nDoctor: Can you confirm whether your weight loss over the past six months has been 10% or more of your original body weight, based on the measurements we have?\n\nPatient: I’m sorry… I don’t think I can honestly confirm the 10% number myself.\n\nWhat I can say is that occupational health sent me here because they said my weight is dangerously low for someone doing my job—running presses and fabrication all day. I didn’t really think of it that way. I just thought I was getting leaner, more disciplined, maybe.\n\nSo… if the measurements show that it’s 10% or more, then I’ll accept that, but I don’t want to overstate it without you checking the numbers.\n\nDoctor: Based on the measurements we have, can you confirm whether your weight loss over the past six months has been 10% or more of your original body weight?\n\nPatient: I’m sorry, I still don’t think I can confirm that myself.\n\nIf the measurements you have show it’s 10% or more, then… I guess that’s what it is. I’m not trying to be difficult—I just don’t want to give the wrong answer or make it sound worse than it is. Could you tell me what the numbers show?\n\nDoctor: Based on the measurements we reviewed together, has your weight loss over the past six months been 10% or more of your original body weight?\n\nPatient: I… I know occupational health said it was dangerously low, especially for the kind of work I do with the presses and fabrication. I guess I didn’t really see it that way at first—I thought I was just getting leaner, more controlled.\n\nSo if the measurements we reviewed show it’s 10% or more, then… yes, I can accept that. I still feel a little embarrassed saying it, but I’m not trying to argue with the numbers.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me a bit more about how you feel when you eat more than your usual small amounts—what goes through your mind, and does it increase your anxiety significantly?\n\nPatient: I mean… I wouldn’t say I never eat, exactly. I do try to keep it pretty controlled, because otherwise I start feeling like the whole day is getting away from me.\n\nMost days it’s coffee before the early shift, then maybe half a protein bar at lunch, and later, after yoga, I’ll have a small bowl of vegetables. If I eat more than that, even if it’s not that much more, I get really anxious—like I’ve messed up or lost control somehow.\n\nI know that probably sounds a bit extreme when I say it out loud. I’m not trying to be difficult… it just feels safer when I keep it small.\n\nDoctor: If you’re someone who should be having regular menstrual periods, have you stopped having your period for at least three months?\n\nPatient: I’m… not completely sure how to answer that. I haven’t been tracking it as carefully as I probably should.\n\nI don’t think I can confidently say “yes, it’s been at least three months.” My periods have never been something I’m great at monitoring, and I’d rather not guess and give you the wrong answer. Is that okay?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I didn’t really see it that way—I thought I was just getting leaner, more disciplined, I guess.\n\nBut yes, if you think looking at the numbers together is the right thing, I’m okay with that.\n\nDoctor: Can you confirm whether your weight loss over the past six months has been 10% or more of your original body weight, based on the measurements we have?\n\nPatient: I’m sorry… I don’t think I can honestly confirm the 10% number myself.\n\nWhat I can say is that occupational health sent me here because they said my weight is dangerously low for someone doing my job—running presses and fabrication all day. I didn’t really think of it that way. I just thought I was getting leaner, more disciplined, maybe.\n\nSo… if the measurements show that it’s 10% or more, then I’ll accept that, but I don’t want to overstate it without you checking the numbers.\n\nDoctor: Based on the measurements we have, can you confirm whether your weight loss over the past six months has been 10% or more of your original body weight?\n\nPatient: I’m sorry, I still don’t think I can confirm that myself.\n\nIf the measurements you have show it’s 10% or more, then… I guess that’s what it is. I’m not trying to be difficult—I just don’t want to give the wrong answer or make it sound worse than it is. Could you tell me what the numbers show?\n\nDoctor: Based on the measurements we reviewed together, has your weight loss over the past six months been 10% or more of your original body weight?\n\nPatient: I… I know occupational health said it was dangerously low, especially for the kind of work I do with the presses and fabrication. I guess I didn’t really see it that way at first—I thought I was just getting leaner, more controlled.\n\nSo if the measurements we reviewed show it’s 10% or more, then… yes, I can accept that. I still feel a little embarrassed saying it, but I’m not trying to argue with the numbers.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me a bit more about how you feel when you eat more than your usual small amounts—what goes through your mind, and does it increase your anxiety significantly?\n\nPatient: I mean… I wouldn’t say I never eat, exactly. I do try to keep it pretty controlled, because otherwise I start feeling like the whole day is getting away from me.\n\nMost days it’s coffee before the early shift, then maybe half a protein bar at lunch, and later, after yoga, I’ll have a small bowl of vegetables. If I eat more than that, even if it’s not that much more, I get really anxious—like I’ve messed up or lost control somehow.\n\nI know that probably sounds a bit extreme when I say it out loud. I’m not trying to be difficult… it just feels safer when I keep it small.\n\nDoctor: If you’re someone who should be having regular menstrual periods, have you stopped having your period for at least three months?\n\nPatient: I’m… not completely sure how to answer that. I haven’t been tracking it as carefully as I probably should.\n\nI don’t think I can confidently say “yes, it’s been at least three months.” My periods have never been something I’m great at monitoring, and I’d rather not guess and give you the wrong answer. Is that okay?\n\nDoctor: Have you stopped having your period for at least three months, if you're someone who should be having regular menstrual periods?\n\nPatient: I’m sorry, I still don’t think I can answer that definitely. I haven’t kept close enough track to say it’s been three months or more.\n\nI know that’s probably not the most helpful answer… I can try to pay more attention going forward, if that’s okay.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. If we have the measurements in front of us, I’m okay looking at them with you, but I don’t want to say “yes” if I’m not sure.\n\nI know it’s been enough that people are concerned, but the 10% part… I’d rather you help me figure that out from the numbers, if that’s alright.\n\nDoctor: Can we look at your weight measurements together now to determine if your weight loss over the past six months has been 10% or more of your body weight?\n\nPatient: Yes, we can look at them now.\n\nI’m… a little embarrassed, honestly, because occupational health already made a big deal out of it. They said my weight is dangerously low for someone running presses and doing fabrication all day. I didn’t really see it that way—I thought I was just getting leaner, more disciplined, I guess.\n\nBut yes, if you think looking at the numbers together is the right thing, I’m okay with that.\n\nDoctor: Can you confirm whether your weight loss over the past six months has been 10% or more of your original body weight, based on the measurements we have?\n\nPatient: I’m sorry… I don’t think I can honestly confirm the 10% number myself.\n\nWhat I can say is that occupational health sent me here because they said my weight is dangerously low for someone doing my job—running presses and fabrication all day. I didn’t really think of it that way. I just thought I was getting leaner, more disciplined, maybe.\n\nSo… if the measurements show that it’s 10% or more, then I’ll accept that, but I don’t want to overstate it without you checking the numbers.\n\nDoctor: Based on the measurements we have, can you confirm whether your weight loss over the past six months has been 10% or more of your original body weight?\n\nPatient: I’m sorry, I still don’t think I can confirm that myself.\n\nIf the measurements you have show it’s 10% or more, then… I guess that’s what it is. I’m not trying to be difficult—I just don’t want to give the wrong answer or make it sound worse than it is. Could you tell me what the numbers show?\n\nDoctor: Based on the measurements we reviewed together, has your weight loss over the past six months been 10% or more of your original body weight?\n\nPatient: I… I know occupational health said it was dangerously low, especially for the kind of work I do with the presses and fabrication. I guess I didn’t really see it that way at first—I thought I was just getting leaner, more controlled.\n\nSo if the measurements we reviewed show it’s 10% or more, then… yes, I can accept that. I still feel a little embarrassed saying it, but I’m not trying to argue with the numbers.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me a bit more about how you feel when you eat more than your usual small amounts—what goes through your mind, and does it increase your anxiety significantly?\n\nPatient: I mean… I wouldn’t say I never eat, exactly. I do try to keep it pretty controlled, because otherwise I start feeling like the whole day is getting away from me.\n\nMost days it’s coffee before the early shift, then maybe half a protein bar at lunch, and later, after yoga, I’ll have a small bowl of vegetables. If I eat more than that, even if it’s not that much more, I get really anxious—like I’ve messed up or lost control somehow.\n\nI know that probably sounds a bit extreme when I say it out loud. I’m not trying to be difficult… it just feels safer when I keep it small.\n\nDoctor: If you’re someone who should be having regular menstrual periods, have you stopped having your period for at least three months?\n\nPatient: I’m… not completely sure how to answer that. I haven’t been tracking it as carefully as I probably should.\n\nI don’t think I can confidently say “yes, it’s been at least three months.” My periods have never been something I’m great at monitoring, and I’d rather not guess and give you the wrong answer. Is that okay?\n\nDoctor: Have you stopped having your period for at least three months, if you're someone who should be having regular menstrual periods?\n\nPatient: I’m sorry, I still don’t think I can answer that definitely. I haven’t kept close enough track to say it’s been three months or more.\n\nI know that’s probably not the most helpful answer… I can try to pay more attention going forward, if that’s okay.\n\nDoctor: Have you gone without having your period for three months or more, even though you are someone who would normally have regular menstrual cycles?\n\nPatient: I’m sorry, I know you’re asking clearly and I’m probably not being very helpful. I just… I honestly don’t know for sure.\n\nI haven’t kept track well enough to say it’s been three months or more. I don’t want to say yes if I’m not certain. I can start writing it down, if that would help?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you consider yourself to be overweight or do you have a fear of gaining weight, even though others might tell you that you're underweight?\n\nPatient: I don’t really think of myself as… overweight, exactly. I know people are saying I’m too thin.\n\nBut if the scale goes up even a little, I get really scared. Like I’ve lost the control I worked so hard to keep, and I’m just going to keep gaining and end up heavy again. I know that probably sounds dramatic… but that’s what happens in my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself sick, use laxatives, or exercise excessively to control your weight or keep from gaining any weight?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so. I mean, I don’t make myself sick or anything like that. And laxatives—no, that’s not something I do.\n\nExercise… I do yoga, but I wouldn’t call it excessive. It helps me feel settled. I know maybe other people might see things differently, but I’m not trying to do anything extreme. Is that… is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you lost more than 10% of your body weight in the past six months, either intentionally or without trying?\n\nPatient: I’m… not totally sure about the percentage. I don’t really calculate it that way.\n\nI know my weight has gone down some over the last few months, but I don’t know if it’s more than 10%. I could check, if that would help. I don’t want to give you the wrong answer.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me approximately how much weight you’ve lost in the past six months, and whether it’s been 10% or more of your body weight?\n\nPatient: I’m sorry, I don’t really know the exact number. I haven’t been keeping it in a way where I can say, like, “it’s this many pounds.”\n\nI know it’s gone down, and people have noticed, but whether it’s 10% or more… I don’t want to guess and give you the wrong answer. I could look back at my weights if that would be okay.\n\nDoctor: Would it be okay if we looked back at your weight measurements together to see if the amount you’ve lost in the past six months is around 10% or more of your body weight?\n\nPatient: Yes… that would be okay.\n\nI mean, occupational health is actually the reason I’m here—they were saying my weight is dangerously low for the kind of work I do, running presses and doing fabrication all day. I honestly thought I was just getting leaner, or more disciplined, not… dangerous.\n\nSo yes, we can look at the measurements together. I’d probably feel better not trying to estimate it on my own, if that’s alright.\n\nDoctor: Can you confirm if your weight loss over the past six months has been 10% or more of your body weight, based on the measurements we can review?\n\nPatient: I… I can’t really confirm it myself just from memory. If we have the measurements in front of us, I’m okay looking at them with you, but I don’t want to say “yes” if I’m not sure.\n\nI know it’s been enough that people are concerned, but the 10% part… I’d rather you help me figure that out from the numbers, if that’s alright.\n\nDoctor: Can we look at your weight measurements together now to determine if your weight loss over the past six months has been 10% or more of your body weight?\n\nPatient: Yes, we can look at them now.\n\nI’m… a little embarrassed, honestly, because occupational health already made a big deal out of it. They said my weight is dangerously low for someone running presses and doing fabrication all day. I didn’t really see it that way—I thought I was just getting leaner, more disciplined, I guess.\n\nBut yes, if you think looking at the numbers together is the right thing, I’m okay with that.\n\nDoctor: Can you confirm whether your weight loss over the past six months has been 10% or more of your original body weight, based on the measurements we have?\n\nPatient: I’m sorry… I don’t think I can honestly confirm the 10% number myself.\n\nWhat I can say is that occupational health sent me here because they said my weight is dangerously low for someone doing my job—running presses and fabrication all day. I’m not trying to be difficult—I just don’t want to give the wrong answer or make it sound worse than it is. Could you tell me what the numbers show?\n\nDoctor: Based on the measurements we reviewed together, has your weight loss over the past six months been 10% or more of your original body weight?\n\nPatient: I… I know occupational health said it was dangerously low, especially for the kind of work I do with the presses and fabrication. I guess I didn’t really see it that way at first—I thought I was just getting leaner, more controlled.\n\nSo if the measurements we reviewed show it’s 10% or more, then… yes, I can accept that. I still feel a little embarrassed saying it, but I’m not trying to argue with the numbers.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me a bit more about how you feel when you eat more than your usual small amounts—what goes through your mind, and does it increase your anxiety significantly?\n\nPatient: I mean… I wouldn’t say I never eat, exactly. I’m not trying to be difficult… it just feels safer when I keep it small.\n\nDoctor: If you’re someone who should be having regular menstrual periods, have you stopped having your period for at least three months?\n\nPatient: I’m… not completely sure how to answer that. I haven’t been tracking it as carefully as I probably should.\n\nI don’t think I can confidently say “yes, it’s been at least three months.” My periods have never been something I’m great at monitoring, and I’d rather not guess and give you the wrong answer.
